1Number Systems & Computer Arithmetic

Signed number representationCore

Notes

A computer only has 0s and 1s. To represent negative numbers, we use one of three schemes on an n-bit word. GATE almost only cares about the third one.

Reference table
SchemeHow -5 (4-bit) looksProblem
Sign-magnitude1101 (sign bit + magnitude 101)two zeros (+0, -0), messy arithmetic
1's complement1010 (flip all bits of 5=0101)still two zeros, needs "end-around carry"
2's complement1011 (flip bits, +1)none — this is what real CPUs use
Formula / Rule
Rule of thumb
To get -x in 2's complement: flip every bit of x, then add 1. To go back from a negative 2's complement number to decimal: flip every bit, add 1, put a minus sign in front.
Range you must memorize
Key point
For an n-bit signed 2's complement number: 
Range = -2^(n-1)  to  +2^(n-1) - 1
So for n = 8: -128 to +127. Notice it's asymmetric — one extra negative number, because there's only one zero.
Formula / Rule
Worked numerical (GATE style)
Q: What is the range of integers representable in 12-bit 2's complement form?
A: n=12 → from −2¹¹ to +2¹¹−1 = −2048 to +2047.
Addition, subtraction & overflow
Notes

2's complement lets you subtract by adding the negative. Just add the bit patterns normally and discard any carry-out of the leftmost bit.

Worked example
Worked numerical
Q: Using 5-bit 2's complement, compute 7 − 3.
A: 7 = 00111, −3 = 11101 (flip 3=00011 → 11100, +1 → 11101).
Add: 00111 + 11101 = 100100 → discard the leading carry → 00100 = 4. Correct!
Common trap
Overflow detection rule
Overflow happens only when adding two numbers of the same sign and the result has the opposite sign. Formally: overflow = carry into MSB XOR carry out of MSB. GATE loves asking "does this addition overflow?" — check the sign bits first, don't compute the whole thing if you don't have to.
Worked example
Worked numerical
Q: In 4-bit 2's complement, does 5 + 4 overflow?
A: 5=0101, 4=0100. Sum = 1001 = −7 in 4-bit. Both operands positive, result negative → overflow (the true sum 9 is above the 4-bit signed maximum of +7, so it cannot be represented).

Floating point: IEEE 754Core

Notes

Fixed-point (2's complement) can't represent very large numbers or fractions efficiently. IEEE 754 splits a number into sign, exponent, mantissa — like scientific notation in binary.

Single precision (32-bit) layout
Layout

31 S 30 E … E 23 M … M 0 M

Notes

Sign — 1 bitExponent — 8 bitsMantissa — 23 bits

Key point
Value = (-1)^S × 1.M × 2^(E - 127)
127 is the bias — it's added to the true exponent so the stored exponent is always non-negative. For double precision: 1 sign + 11 exponent (bias 1023) + 52 mantissa = 64 bits.
Worked example
Worked numerical
Q: Represent 10.25 in IEEE-754 single precision.
A:
1. Binary: 10.25 = 1010.01
2. Normalize: 1.01001 × 2³
3. Sign = 0 (positive)
4. Exponent = 3 + 127 = 130 = 10000010
5. Mantissa (drop leading 1, pad to 23 bits) = 01001000000000000000000
Final: 0 10000010 01001000000000000000000
Common trap
Special values to remember
E=0, M=0 → zero  |  E=0, M≠0 → denormal (subnormal) number  |  E=255 (all 1s), M=0 → ±infinity  |  E=255, M≠0 → NaN. GATE frequently asks "what does this bit pattern represent" — check E first.

Carry vs overflow, sign extension, and other codesHigh-yield

Reference table
FlagMeaningTest
Carry (C)Unsigned result does not fitCarry out of the MSB
Overflow (V)Signed result does not fitCarry into the MSB XOR carry out of the MSB
Worked example
Worked example
Q: In 4 bits, add 1111 and 0001. Report carry and overflow.
A: 1111 + 0001 = 1 0000, so the result is 0000 with carry = 1. Unsigned, that is 15 + 1 = 16, which does not fit. Signed, it is (−1) + 1 = 0, which is correct: carry into the MSB = 1 and carry out = 1, so XOR = 0 and overflow = 0. The same bit pattern can set one flag and not the other.
Key point
Sign extension (widening a 2's-complement number) copies the sign bit into the new high bits: 1101 (−3) in 8 bits is 11111101, still −3. Unsigned numbers are zero-extended instead.

Other codes. BCD: each decimal digit in 4 bits (59 → 0101 1001). Excess-3: BCD digit + 3. Gray code: consecutive values differ in exactly one bit; for 3 bits it runs 000, 001, 011, 010, 110, 111, 101, 100, and Gray = binary XOR (binary >> 1).

2Instruction Set & Addressing Modes

Instruction formats: how many "addresses"?Core

Notes

An instruction = opcode (what to do) + operands (who to do it to). Older CPUs are classified by how many memory addresses an instruction can reference:

Reference table
TypeExample (for C = A + B)Used in
3-addressADD C, A, BRISC-like / register machines
2-addressMOV C, A then ADD C, Bx86-style CISC
1-address (accumulator)LOAD A; ADD B; STORE Csimple/old machines
0-address (stack)PUSH A; PUSH B; ADD; POP Cstack machines, JVM
GATE favourite
GATE trap
Fewer addresses per instruction ⇒ more instructions needed to do the same job, but each instruction is shorter. GATE often asks you to count total instructions or total bits for a given expression across these formats.

Addressing modes — where is the actual operand?Core

Notes

The instruction doesn't always contain the value directly. The addressing mode tells the CPU how to compute the Effective Address (EA) — where in memory/registers the real data sits.

Reference table
ModeInstruction saysEffective Address / operand
ImmediateADD R1, #5operand IS 5 (no memory access)
DirectADD R1, [200]EA = 200
IndirectADD R1, [[200]]EA = contents of address 200 (memory holds a pointer)
RegisterADD R1, R2operand is inside R2, no memory trip
Register indirectADD R1, [R2]EA = value inside R2
IndexedADD R1, [R2+X]EA = R2 + index register X (array access)
Base registerADD R1, [Base+disp]EA = base reg + displacement (relocatable code)
RelativeBR [PC+disp]EA = PC + displacement (branches)
Auto-increment/decrementADD R1, [R2]+EA = R2, then R2 = R2+size (array traversal)
Flow

Indirect: Instruction → points to Address 200 → Mem[200] = Address 350 → Mem[350] = the real operand

Worked example
Worked numerical
Q: An instruction uses indirect addressing with the address field = 300. Mem[300] = 500, Mem[500] = 42. What is the effective operand?
A: Indirect ⇒ EA = Mem[300] = 500. Operand = Mem[500] = 42. (Indirect addressing costs one extra memory reference compared to direct — this is exactly why GATE loves timing questions: "if memory access = 100ns and indirect mode used, extra time = 100ns per instruction".)
Worked example
Worked numerical
Q: Register R1 = 2000. Instruction: LOAD R2, 20[R1] using indexed addressing, and Mem[2020] = 77. Find R2 after execution.
A: EA = R1 + 20 = 2000 + 20 = 2020. R2 ← Mem[2020] = 77.
RISC vs CISC (quick contrast, often 1 mark)
Key point
RISC
Few, simple, fixed-length instructions. Mostly register-to-register. Simple addressing modes. More instructions needed, but each is fast (1 cycle typical) — pipelines well.
Key point
CISC
Many complex, variable-length instructions. Instructions can directly operate on memory. Fewer instructions per program, but each may take several cycles.

Expanding opcodesCore

Notes

A fixed-length instruction can hold more instruction types by reusing unused opcode patterns as a prefix for a longer opcode. Each time the opcode grows, the address fields shrink to make room.

Worked example
Worked numerical
Q: A 16-bit instruction has 4-bit address fields. The machine needs 14 three-address instructions, 30 two-address instructions, and as many one-address instructions as possible. How many one-address instructions fit?
A: 
Level 1 (3 addresses): 4-bit opcode, 3 × 4-bit addresses   → 16 patterns, use 14, 2 left
Level 2 (2 addresses): 8-bit opcode, 2 × 4-bit addresses   → 2 × 16 = 32 patterns, use 30, 2 left
Level 3 (1 address)  : 12-bit opcode, 1 × 4-bit address    → 2 × 16 = 32 patterns
So up to 32 one-address instructions. If only 31 were used, 1 pattern would remain: 1 × 16 = 16 zero-address instructions with a 16-bit opcode.

Byte ordering (endianness)High-yield

Notes

For the 32-bit value 0x12345678 stored at address 100: big-endian keeps the most significant byte first (100: 12, 101: 34, 102: 56, 103: 78); little-endian keeps the least significant byte first (100: 78, 101: 56, 102: 34, 103: 12). It matters when data is shared between machines or read byte by byte.

3Design of the Arithmetic Logic Unit (ALU)

Adders: the building blockCore

Key point
Ripple Carry Adder (RCA)
n 1-bit full adders chained; carry "ripples" from LSB to MSB. Simple, but slow — the MSB must wait for every earlier carry.
Delay = n × Δ (Δ = delay of 1 full adder).
Key point
Carry Look-Ahead Adder (CLA)
Computes all carries in parallel using extra gates (generate/propagate logic), so it doesn't wait for ripples. Faster but more hardware.
Worked example
Worked numerical (classic GATE pattern)
Q: A 16-bit adder is built from 4-bit carry-look-ahead adder (CLA) blocks connected by ripple carry between blocks. Each 4-bit CLA block has delay 10 ns to produce its sum and its carry-out. Find the total worst-case delay.
A: There are 16/4 = 4 blocks. Block 1 finishes in 10 ns and passes its carry-out to block 2, which then takes another 10 ns, and so on. Total delay = 4 × 10 = 40 ns. (Contrast: if all 4 blocks used full look-ahead across blocks too, delay would be much closer to a single block's 10 ns.)

Multiplication: Booth's AlgorithmCore

Notes

Booth's algorithm multiplies signed 2's-complement numbers by scanning pairs of bits of the multiplier and deciding to add, subtract, or do nothing to a running partial product, then arithmetic-shift right.

Key point
Rule, looking at multiplier bit Q₀ and the bit just shifted out Q₋₁:
Q₀ Q₋₁Action
0 0no operation, just shift
0 1Add multiplicand, then shift
1 0Subtract multiplicand, then shift
1 1no operation, just shift
Worked example
Worked numerical
Q: Multiply 3 × (−4) using Booth's algorithm (4-bit).
A: Multiplicand M = 3 = 0011, Multiplier Q = −4 = 1100, Q₋₁ = 0, AC = 0000. Each step: check Q₀Q₋₁, do the add/sub, then arithmetic-shift AC:Q:Q₋₁ right by 1. 
Step  Q0Q-1  Action     AC after action   AC:Q:Q-1 after shift
1      0 0   none       0000              AC=0000 Q=0110 Q-1=0
2      0 0   none       0000              AC=0000 Q=0011 Q-1=0
3      1 0   AC=AC-M    1101              AC=1110 Q=1001 Q-1=1
4      1 1   none       1110              AC=1111 Q=0100 Q-1=1
Final product = AC:Q = 1111 0100 (8-bit 2's complement) = −12 — matching 3 × (−4). ✔
Common trap
Why Booth's matters
It handles negative multipliers automatically and can skip strings of 0s or 1s, making multiplication faster than naive shift-and-add. GATE frequently gives you a partial trace and asks you to fill in the next AC or Q value — memorize the 4-row table above cold.

DivisionHigh-yield

Notes

Two standard hardware algorithms, both repeat "shift, subtract divisor, check sign, restore if needed":

Key point
Restoring division
Subtract divisor; if remainder goes negative, add divisor back (restore) and quotient bit = 0. Else quotient bit = 1. Simple but the extra "restore" step costs time.
Key point
Non-restoring division
Never restores — instead alternates add/subtract based on the sign of the previous remainder. Same number of steps, no wasted restore cycle, so it's faster.

Carry look-ahead equationsCore

Notes

For each bit position define generate Gᵢ = Aᵢ·Bᵢ (this bit makes a carry by itself) and propagate Pᵢ = Aᵢ ⊕ Bᵢ (this bit passes an incoming carry on). Then Cᵢ₊₁ = Gᵢ + Pᵢ·Cᵢ and Sumᵢ = Pᵢ ⊕ Cᵢ. Expanding the recurrence removes the wait for ripples: C₁ = G₀ + P₀C₀, C₂ = G₁ + P₁G₀ + P₁P₀C₀, and so on. Each carry is a two-level AND-OR of G, P and C₀, so its delay does not grow with the bit position (but the gate fan-in does, which is why large adders use 4-bit blocks).

4Design of the Control Unit

The instruction cycle in micro-operationsCore

Notes

Every instruction is really a short sequence of register transfers. GATE loves writing these out; you should be able to read/write them fluently.

Key point
Example — the Fetch cycle (same for every instruction): 
MAR ← PC
MBR ← Memory[MAR] , PC ← PC + 1
IR  ← MBR
Then Decode reads the opcode in IR, and Execute runs instruction-specific micro-ops, e.g. for ADD R1, [500]: 
MAR ← 500
MBR ← Memory[MAR]
R1  ← R1 + MBR

Two ways to build the control unitCore

Reference table
HardwiredMicroprogrammed
How signals are generatedFixed combinational logic / FSM built from gatesA tiny stored "control program" (microprogram) in a Control Store (ROM)
SpeedFast — pure logic delaySlower — an extra memory read per micro-instruction
FlexibilityHard to change — needs a re-design/re-wiringEasy to change — just rewrite the control store contents
Best forRISC, simple instruction sets, speed-critical CPUsCISC, complex/large instruction sets
Cost / complexityComplex to design, cheap to runSimple, systematic design; needs control-store memory
Microinstruction format
Notes

Each word in the control store is a microinstruction: a row of control-signal bits plus the address of the next microinstruction.

Layout

Control field which signals to assert (e.g. MAR-load, ALU-add, Mem-read) Address field address of next micro-instruction

Key point
Horizontal microprogramming
One bit per control signal — wide word, no decoding needed, but uses lots of control-store space. Faster (parallel signals).
Key point
Vertical microprogramming
Signals are encoded (like a mini opcode) — narrow word, needs a decoder, saves space but slightly slower.
Worked example
Worked numerical
Q: A control store must hold 256 microinstructions, each needing to specify one of 8 possible micro-operations per field, using vertical microprogramming with 3 independent fields. How many bits per microinstruction (ignoring the next-address field)?
A: Each field encodes 8 choices → needs log₂8 = 3 bits. With 3 independent fields → 3 × 3 = 9 bits for control, plus log₂256 = 8 bits for the next-address field → total 17 bits/microinstruction.
GATE favourite
GATE angle to remember
Questions often give you total control store size and ask for the number of address bits (log₂ of total microinstructions), or give you the number of control signals and ask you to compare horizontal (1 bit/signal) vs vertical (encoded, fewer bits) word width.

Data path: single-bus vs three-busCore

Notes

In a single-bus data path only one register can drive the bus at a time, so the ALU needs a holding register Y for one operand and an output register Z for the result. In a three-bus data path two buses feed the ALU and the third carries the result back, so an ALU operation takes one step.

Worked example
Worked example — R3 ← R1 + R2
Single bus (3 control steps):
  1. R1out, Yin
  2. R2out, Add (ALU uses Y and the bus), Zin
  3. Zout, R3in
Three bus (1 step): R1 → bus A, R2 → bus B, ALU result → bus C → R3
The instruction fetch on a single bus also takes 3 steps: (1) PCout, MARin, Read, Select4, Add, Zin; (2) Zout, PCin, Yin, wait for memory; (3) MDRout, IRin. More buses mean fewer steps per instruction but more hardware.

5Memory Hierarchy & Cache Mapping

The hierarchyCore

Flow

Registers (fastest, smallest) → Cache (L1/L2/L3) → Main Memory (RAM) → Secondary storage (slowest, biggest)

Notes

The idea: keep frequently-used data in the fast, small, expensive memory close to the CPU (locality of reference), and fall back to slower memory only on a miss.

Average Memory Access Time (AMAT)Core

Key point
AMAT = Hit time + Miss rate × Miss penalty
Or in terms of hit ratio h: Effective Access Time = h × Tc + (1 − h) × (Tc + Tm), where Tc = cache access time, Tm = main memory access time.
Worked example
Worked numerical
Q: Cache access time = 10 ns, main memory access time = 100 ns, hit ratio = 0.9. Find effective access time.
A: EAT = 0.9×10 + 0.1×(10+100) = 9 + 11 = 20 ns.

How an address maps into cache — 3 schemesCore

Notes

Every physical address is split into three fields: Tag | Set/Line index | Block offset. The scheme decides how many places one memory block can go.

Layout

Tag Set / Line index Block offset

Reference table
MappingRuleIndex field
Direct mappedEach memory block → exactly ONE cache linepresent — determines the single line
Fully associativeA block can go in ANY cache linenone — compare tag against all lines
Set-associative (n-way)A block maps to ONE set, but can sit in any of the n lines in that setpresent — picks the set, then search n lines
Worked example
Worked numerical (very common GATE pattern)
Q: Main memory = 4 GB (32-bit address), cache = 64 KB, block size = 32 bytes, direct-mapped. Find tag, index, and offset bit widths.
A:
Offset bits = log₂(block size) = log₂32 = 5 bits
Number of cache lines = cache size / block size = 65536/32 = 2048 lines → index bits = log₂2048 = 11 bits
Tag bits = total address bits − index − offset = 32 − 11 − 5 = 16 bits
Worked example
Worked numerical — 4-way set associative
Q: Same cache (64 KB, 32-byte blocks) but organized as 4-way set-associative. Find the index bits.
A: Number of sets = lines / ways = 2048 / 4 = 512 sets → index bits = log₂512 = 9 bits. Tag bits become 32 − 9 − 5 = 18 bits (bigger tag because fewer sets to search).
GATE favourite
Replacement & write policy — quick recall
When a set is full, a block must be evicted: LRU (least recently used) is the GATE favorite, also FIFO / random. Write policy: write-through (update memory immediately, simple, slower) vs write-back (update memory only on eviction, needs a "dirty bit", faster).

Write misses and cache miss typesCore

Reference table
Policy pairOn a write hitOn a write miss
Write-through + no-write-allocateUpdate cache and memoryWrite memory only; the block is not brought into the cache
Write-back + write-allocateUpdate the cache and set the dirty bitFetch the block into the cache, then write it there
Notes

A write-back cache writes memory only when a dirty block is evicted, which saves bus traffic, but a replacement may then cost an extra memory write. Miss types (the 3 Cs): compulsory (first access to a block), capacity (the working set exceeds the cache) and conflict (too many blocks map to the same set; a fully associative cache has none).

Multi-level cache access timeCore

Notes

AMAT = t₁ + m₁·(t₂ + m₂·t_mem) where mᵢ is the miss rate of level i (local miss rate) and tᵢ its access time.

Worked example
Worked numerical
Q: L1: 1 ns, miss rate 10%. L2: 10 ns, local miss rate 20%. Memory: 100 ns. Find the average access time.
A: AMAT = 1 + 0.1 × (10 + 0.2 × 100) = 1 + 0.1 × 30 = 4 ns.

Memory interleaving and memory typesHigh-yield

Notes

With m-way low-order interleaving, consecutive addresses fall in different banks (bank = address mod m), so m accesses can overlap. Example: 4 banks, 100 ns per bank access: fetching 8 consecutive words takes 2 × 100 = 200 ns (two rounds of 4 parallel accesses), against 800 ns from a single bank, assuming no bank conflicts.

Reference table
SRAMDRAM
CellFlip-flop (about 6 transistors)1 transistor + 1 capacitor
RefreshNot neededNeeded (charge leaks)
Speed / cost / densityFaster, costlier, less denseSlower, cheaper, denser
Typical useCacheMain memory

Memory interfacing — chips, decoding & expansionCore

1. A memory chip, as the CPU sees it
Notes

A chip of N × w (N words, w bits per word) has log₂N address lines, w data lines, a chip select (CS) and read/write controls (RD/WR or OE/WE). CS is usually active-low: with CS inactive the chip ignores the address and its data lines float (tri-state), so many chips can share one data bus.

Reference table
ChipAddress linesData linesCapacity
1K × 81081 KB
16K × 11412 KB
4M × 82284 MB
2. Address decoding & chip select
Flow

CPU address A(k−1) … A(m) (high bits) → Decoder → CS of one chip / chip group

Flow

CPU address A(m−1) … A0 (low bits) → Address pins of every chip in parallel

Key point
Rule. Low bits (as many as the chip has address lines) go to all chips and pick the word inside a chip. High bits go to a decoder (or logic) that raises exactly one CS and picks which chip. The decoder is enabled only for the address bits that must have a fixed value.
Common trap
Trap — partial decoding
If some high address bits are not used in the decoder, they become don’t-cares and the same chip answers at several address ranges (aliasing / “mirroring”). Full decoding uses every high bit and gives a unique address for every location.
3. Expansion — two independent directions
Reference table
ExpansionWhat growsHow the chips connect
Word-size (width)Bits per wordChips side by side: same address, same CS; data lines are concatenated (e.g. two 8-bit chips → 16-bit word)
Capacity (depth)Number of wordsChips stacked: low bits to all, high bits → decoder → separate CS; data lines are shared on the bus
Formula / Rule
Chips needed = (N × W) / (n × w)          memory N × W built from chips n × w
Columns (word-size) = W / w               Rows (capacity) = N / n
Decoder inputs = log₂(Rows)               Rows = number of CS lines
4. Representative problems
Note
Problem 1 — expansion in both directions
Q: Build a 4K × 16 memory from 1K × 4 chips. How many chips, how are they arranged, and how are the address bits used?
A: Chips = (4096 × 16)/(1024 × 4) = 16. Columns = 16/4 = 4 chips side by side give the 16-bit word; rows = 4096/1024 = 4 groups give the depth. The 12-bit address splits as: A9–A0 (10 bits) to every chip; A11–A10 (2 bits) to a 2-to-4 decoder whose four outputs are the CS of the four rows.
Formula / Rule
Problem 2 — address ranges from a decoder
Q: A CPU has a 16-bit address bus. Four 2K × 8 chips are placed contiguously from 0000H, with A15–A13 required to be 000. Give each chip’s range.
A: Each chip has 11 address lines (A10–A0) and covers 800H bytes. A15–A13 = 000 enables a 2-to-4 decoder on A12, A11: 
Chip 0 (A12A11 = 00): 0000H – 07FFH
Chip 1 (A12A11 = 01): 0800H – 0FFFH
Chip 2 (A12A11 = 10): 1000H – 17FFH
Chip 3 (A12A11 = 11): 1800H – 1FFFH       total 8 KB
Because A15–A13 are decoded, the chips do not alias elsewhere in the 64 KB space. If A15–A13 were ignored, the same 8 KB would repeat 8 times (partial decoding).
Note
Problem 3 — decoder sizing
Q: A 16-bit address bus drives 64 KB of memory made of 8K × 8 chips. How many chips and which decoder?
A: Chips = 64K/8K = 8 (all in one column, as the word is already 8 bits). Each chip needs 13 address lines (A12–A0); the remaining 3 bits A15–A13 feed a 3-to-8 decoder whose 8 outputs are the CS lines. Chip i occupies i × 2000H to i × 2000H + 1FFFH.
Common trap
Byte-addressable memory on a wider bus
On a byte-addressable machine with a 32-bit data bus, the memory is built as 4 byte-wide banks. The 2 lowest address bits (A1–A0) choose the byte within the word and are not sent to the chips; the chips see A(k−1)–A2. Read the question to see whether the “word” is the byte or the bus width.

Virtual memory and the TLBHigh-yield

Notes

A virtual address splits into page number | offset. The page table maps page → frame. Page-table size = (number of virtual pages) × (entry size): a 32-bit virtual address with 4 KB pages has 2²⁰ pages, so with 4-byte entries the table is 2²⁰ × 4 B = 4 MB. A TLB caches recent translations so most accesses skip the page-table lookup in memory. With a single-level page table:

Worked example
Worked numerical
Q: TLB access 10 ns, memory access 100 ns, TLB hit ratio 90%. Find the effective access time.
A: A hit costs 10 + 100 = 110 ns. A miss costs 10 + 100 (page-table read) + 100 (data) = 210 ns. EMAT = 0.9 × 110 + 0.1 × 210 = 99 + 21 = 120 ns.

Secondary storage — disk access timeExtension

Notes

Access time = seek time + rotational latency + transfer time. Average rotational latency is half a rotation; transfer time = (bytes wanted / bytes per track) × rotation time.

Worked example
Worked numerical
Q: Seek time 4 ms, 7200 rpm, 400 sectors per track. Find the time to read one sector.
A: One rotation = 60/7200 s = 8.33 ms, so the average latency is 4.17 ms and one sector passes under the head in 8.33/400 = 0.021 ms. Total = 4 + 4.17 + 0.02 ≈ 8.19 ms.

6I/O Interface: Interrupts & DMA

Key point
Programmed I/O
CPU repeatedly polls the device's status flag in a busy-wait loop. Simple, but wastes CPU cycles.
Key point
Interrupt-driven I/O
CPU carries on with other work; the device raises an interrupt signal only when it's ready. CPU saves state, runs the ISR (interrupt service routine), then resumes. Saves CPU time vs polling, but each transfer still passes through the CPU register-by-register.
Key point
DMA (Direct Memory Access)
A separate DMA controller moves an entire block directly between device and memory, without the CPU touching each word. CPU just sets up (starting address, count, direction) then is interrupted once at the very end. Best for bulk transfers (disk, network).
Cycle stealing
Notes

While a DMA transfer is in progress, the DMA controller "steals" memory bus cycles from the CPU one at a time, so both can (almost) work concurrently — this is why DMA is much more efficient than interrupt-driven I/O for large transfers.

Worked example
Worked numerical
Q: A device transfers data at 1 MB/s. Each interrupt-driven transfer moves 4 bytes and costs the CPU 100 cycles of overhead (context switch + ISR) at 1 GHz. Compare CPU overhead per second against a DMA scheme that interrupts only once per 4 KB block.
A:
Interrupt-driven: transfers/sec = 1,000,000/4 = 250,000 interrupts/sec → overhead = 250,000 × 100 cycles = 25,000,000 cycles/sec = 2.5% of a 1 GHz CPU just on overhead.
DMA: interrupts/sec = 1,000,000/4096 ≈ 244 → overhead ≈ 24,400 cycles/sec — roughly 1000× less CPU overhead.
GATE favourite
GATE angle
Typical questions: given interrupt latency & instruction execution time, compute total time lost to interrupts; or compare polling vs interrupt vs DMA overhead like above. Also know: vectored interrupts (device ID tells CPU where its ISR is) vs non-vectored (fixed single ISR address, must poll to find the source).

Interrupt priority: daisy chain and nestingCore

Notes

When several devices share one interrupt line, the CPU needs a priority scheme. Daisy chain: the interrupt-acknowledge signal passes serially from device to device, so the device nearest the CPU has the highest priority. Parallel priority uses a separate request line per device, a mask register and a priority encoder; it is faster but needs more hardware. With nested interrupts a higher-priority request can interrupt a running ISR while lower-priority ones stay masked. The CPU finishes the current instruction before it responds to an interrupt.

Memory-mapped vs isolated I/OCore

Reference table
Memory-mapped I/OIsolated (port-mapped) I/O
Address spaceDevice registers share the memory address spaceSeparate I/O address space
InstructionsOrdinary load/storeSpecial IN / OUT
CostUses up part of the memory address rangeFull memory range kept; needs an extra control signal

DMA transfer modesCore

Reference table
ModeHow it uses the busCPU effect
Burst (block)Controller keeps the bus for the whole blockCPU is blocked from memory for the entire burst
Cycle stealingTakes the bus for one word at a timeCPU slowed slightly
TransparentUses the bus only when the CPU is not using itNo slowdown, but the transfer may be slower
Worked example
Worked numerical
Q: A device transfers 4 MB/s in 4-byte words using cycle stealing. Each stolen memory cycle lasts 100 ns. What fraction of the memory bus time does DMA use?
A: 4 MB/s ÷ 4 B = 1 million words per second. Each steals 100 ns: 10⁶ × 100 ns = 0.1 s per second = 10% of the bus time, so the CPU keeps the other 90%.

7Instruction Pipelining & Hazards

The classic 5-stage pipelineCore

Key point
IF Instruction Fetch → ID Instruction Decode → EX Execute → MEM Memory access → WB Write back
Reference table
Cycle →123456
Instr 1IFIDEXMEMWB
Instr 2IFIDEXMEMWB
Notes

Without pipelining each instruction takes 5 cycles start-to-finish; with pipelining, once the pipe is full, one instruction completes every cycle.

Speedup formula
Key point
Time (non-pipelined) = n × k
Time (pipelined)      = k + (n − 1)
Speedup = (n × k) / (k + n − 1)
n = number of instructions, k = number of pipeline stages. As n→∞, speedup → k.
Worked example
Worked numerical
Q: A 5-stage pipeline, clock cycle = 2 ns, runs 100 instructions with no stalls. Find total time and speedup vs non-pipelined execution.
A: Pipelined cycles = k + n − 1 = 5 + 99 = 104 → time = 104 × 2 = 208 ns.
Non-pipelined time = n × k × cycle = 100 × 5 × 2 = 1000 ns.
Speedup = 1000 / 208 ≈ 4.8× (close to the ideal max of 5).

Hazards — the reason pipelines stallCore

Reference table
HazardCauseFix
StructuralTwo instructions need the same hardware resource (e.g. one memory port for both instruction fetch and data access) at onceDuplicate hardware (separate I-cache/D-cache), or stall
DataAn instruction needs a result that a previous, still-unfinished instruction hasn't produced yetForwarding/bypassing (feed result directly from EX/MEM stage), or stall
Control (branch)The next instruction to fetch isn't known until a branch is resolved (usually in EX/MEM stage)Branch prediction, delayed branch, or stall until resolved
Worked example
Worked numerical — data hazard with stalls
Q: ADD R1, R2, R3 is immediately followed by SUB R4, R1, R5 in a 5-stage pipeline with no forwarding. R1 is written in WB (cycle 5 of instr 1) but needed by SUB's EX stage. How many stall (bubble) cycles are needed?
A: Without forwarding, SUB's ID stage (where operands are read) must happen no earlier than the cycle right after instr 1's WB. Instr 1: IF ID EX MEM WB (cycles 1-5). Instr 2 naturally would be ID at cycle 3 — but it needs the value only ready after cycle 5. So ID must be delayed to cycle 6 → 3 stall cycles inserted. (With full forwarding from EX/MEM to the next instruction's EX, this typically drops to 0–1 stalls.)
GATE favourite
GATE angle
Questions typically give a sequence of dependent instructions and a pipeline diagram, then ask: total cycles with stalls, or how many bubbles a specific forwarding path removes, or the effect of a branch misprediction (stall cycles = number of stages before branch resolves).

Data hazard types: RAW, WAR, WAWCore

Reference table
HazardMeaningOccurs in a simple in-order 5-stage pipeline?
RAW (true dependency)A later instruction reads a register before an earlier one has written itYes — the main cause of stalls
WAR (anti-dependency)A later instruction writes a register before an earlier one has read itNo — reads happen early, writes late
WAW (output dependency)Two instructions write the same register; the writes could complete out of orderNo — writes stay in order
Notes

WAR and WAW appear with out-of-order execution or instructions of different latency, and are removed by register renaming. With forwarding, an ALU result flows from EX/MEM straight into the next instruction's EX stage, removing the stall for back-to-back ALU instructions. A load followed by a use of the loaded value still costs 1 stall cycle even with forwarding, because the data is only available after MEM.

Branch penalty and predictionCore

Notes

CPI = 1 + (branch frequency) × (fraction mispredicted or stalled) × (penalty in cycles). With 20% branches and a 3-cycle penalty, always stalling gives CPI = 1 + 0.2 × 3 = 1.6; with a predictor that is wrong 40% of the time, CPI = 1 + 0.2 × 0.4 × 3 = 1.24. Techniques: static prediction (always not-taken, or backward-taken/forward-not-taken), delayed branch (the instruction in the delay slot always executes), and dynamic prediction with a branch history table. A 2-bit saturating counter needs two wrong predictions in a row to change its mind, so a loop branch mispredicts only once per loop exit.

Pipeline with unequal stagesCore

Notes

The clock must fit the slowest stage plus the latch delay. A non-pipelined machine takes the sum of the stage delays per instruction.

Worked example
Worked numerical
Q: Four stages take 5, 10, 8 and 7 ns, and each pipeline latch adds 1 ns. Find the speedup for 100 instructions.
A: Clock = 10 + 1 = 11 ns. Pipelined time = (4 + 99) × 11 = 1133 ns. Non-pipelined time = (5 + 10 + 8 + 7) × 100 = 3000 ns. Speedup = 3000 / 1133 ≈ 2.65, far below 4 because the slow stage sets the clock for everyone.

CPU performance: CPI, MIPS and Amdahl's lawHigh-yield

Notes

CPU time = instruction count × CPI × clock cycle time; MIPS = clock rate / (CPI × 10⁶). For a mix, CPI is the frequency-weighted average of the per-class cycle counts. Amdahl's law: if a fraction f of the time is sped up by a factor s, Speedup = 1 / ((1 − f) + f/s), and the limit as s → ∞ is 1/(1 − f).

Worked example
Worked numerical
Q: A 1 GHz CPU runs a mix of 50% ALU (1 cycle), 20% loads (2), 10% stores (2) and 20% branches (3). Find CPI, MIPS and the time for 10⁹ instructions. Separately, if 80% of a program is sped up 4×, what is the overall speedup?
A: CPI = 0.5·1 + 0.2·2 + 0.1·2 + 0.2·3 = 0.5 + 0.4 + 0.2 + 0.6 = 1.7. MIPS = 1000 / 1.7 ≈ 588. Time = 10⁹ × 1.7 × 1 ns = 1.7 s. Amdahl: 1 / (0.2 + 0.8/4) = 1 / 0.4 = 2.5, and even an infinite speedup of that part could give at most 1/0.2 = 5.

Flynn's taxonomy and instruction-level parallelismExtension

Reference table
ClassInstruction streamsData streamsExample
SISD11Classic uniprocessor
SIMD1manyVector/GPU-style units
MISDmany1Rare (fault-tolerant designs)
MIMDmanymanyMulticore, clusters
Notes

A superscalar CPU issues several instructions per cycle (CPI can fall below 1); a VLIW machine has the compiler pack independent operations into one long instruction word.

8Problem → Standard Method Playbook

Method

Instruction Encoding & Datapath

Problem patternStandard methodKey check
Instruction length / opcode fieldsCount total encoding bits and required operand/register/address bits; remainder is opcode/function space.Check whether the question uses fixed or variable/expanding opcode.
Expanding opcodeAllocate short opcodes first; each longer opcode consumes a prefix branch of the code space.Never count all bit patterns independently when prefixes are constrained.
Single-bus datapathTrace one transfer per bus cycle; identify source register → bus → destination and ALU control.Two registers cannot normally drive the same single bus simultaneously.
Multi-bus datapathTrace simultaneous source reads and destination write-back using separate buses.More buses can reduce cycles but add hardware.
EndiannessSplit a multi-byte value into bytes and place the least/most significant byte at the lowest address according to little/big endian.Endianness changes byte order, not bit order within a byte.
Method

Cache & Memory

PatternStandard methodFormula / trap
Cache address splitOffset bits=log₂(block size); number of lines/sets determines index bits; remaining bits are tag.For S-way set associative cache, sets=lines/S.
AMATAMAT=hit time + miss rate×miss penalty.For multi-level cache, expand recursively with conditional probabilities.
Write policyDistinguish write-through/write-back and write-allocate/no-write-allocate.Write-back updates memory on eviction; write-through updates memory on write.
Memory interleavingDistribute consecutive words across banks; determine bank number from address modulo number of banks.Check whether the question assumes low-order or high-order interleaving.
Secondary storageUse block/sector geometry and seek/rotational/transfer components as specified.Don't invent a seek component if the problem gives only transfer time.
Method

Pipeline & Performance

PatternMethodTrap
Pipeline speedupCompare non-pipelined time with pipeline fill + steady-state cycles.For n instructions and k stages, ideal cycles=n+k−1.
Hazard countingIdentify RAW/WAR/WAW and structural/control hazards; then apply forwarding/stalls/branch penalty stated in the question.Forwarding does not remove every RAW hazard.
CPI/performanceCPU time = Instruction Count × CPI × Clock Cycle Time.Weighted CPI if instruction classes have different CPIs.
Amdahl's lawSpeedup=1/[(1−f)+f/s], where f is fraction improved and s is speedup of that fraction.Speedup applies to execution-time fraction, not necessarily instruction fraction.
Method

I/O & Interrupts

PatternStandard methodKey distinction
Programmed I/OCPU polls device status and transfers data.CPU remains involved in waiting.
Interrupt-driven I/ODevice interrupts CPU when service is needed; CPU runs ISR.Know interrupt latency and priority assumptions.
DMADMA controller transfers blocks between device and memory with limited CPU intervention.Distinguish DMA setup time from CPU transfer time.
Priority / daisy chainApply the stated priority ordering to simultaneous requests; daisy chaining gives priority according to physical chain position.Priority is an ordering, not a bandwidth calculation.
Memory-mapped vs isolated I/OCheck whether I/O registers share the memory address space or use a separate I/O address space.This affects instruction/address-space assumptions.
Method

🧮 Representative GATE-style Worked Problems

Problem 1 · Expanding opcode

An ISA has 8-bit instructions. A 4-bit opcode identifies 8 instructions using a prefix scheme. How do you reason about additional opcode space?

Method: Draw the prefix tree: each unused prefix branch can be expanded only if it does not collide with an existing complete opcode.

Takeaway: Count available prefix leaves, not raw 2n patterns.

Problem 2 · Datapath

A single-bus datapath must execute R3←R1+R2. What transfers are required?

Method: Place R1 on the bus → latch into ALU input A; place R2 on bus → ALU adds; place result on bus → load R3.

Takeaway: A single bus generally serializes source transfers.

Problem 3 · Amdahl

40% of execution time is improved by a factor of 4. Find overall speedup.

Method: S=1/[(1−0.4)+0.4/4].

Answer: 1/0.7 = 10/7 ≈ 1.43×.

Problem 4 · Cache

A cache has 64 lines and is 4-way set associative. How many sets?

Method: Sets=64/4.

Answer: 16 sets; therefore index bits=log₂16=4.

Problem 5 · Interleaving

Memory has 4 banks with low-order interleaving. Which bank receives word address A?

Method: bank=A mod 4.

Trap: If high-order interleaving is specified, the mapping is different.

9Last-minute one-liners

Last-minute · formulas & rules
  • Memory interfacing: chips needed = (N×W)/(n×w). Word-size expansion = chips in parallel (same address and CS, data lines concatenated); capacity expansion = chips stacked (high address bits → decoder → CS).
  • Effective address questions require careful attention to base/index/offset semantics and word vs byte addressing.
  • Address bits = tag + set/index bits + block-offset bits; offset bits = log₂(block size in bytes).
  • Pipeline speedup is limited by stage imbalance, register overhead, stalls, and pipeline fill/drain effects.
  • Expanding opcode: each level multiplies leftover patterns by 2address bits freed. Carry = carry out of the MSB (unsigned); overflow = carry into MSB XOR carry out (signed).
  • Multi-level AMAT = t1 + m1·(t2 + m2·t_mem). TLB EMAT (single level) = h·(t_tlb + t_mem) + (1−h)·(t_tlb + 2·t_mem). Disk access = seek + half-rotation + transfer.
  • Pipeline clock = slowest stage + latch delay. CPI = 1 + branch freq × mispredict rate × penalty. CPU time = IC × CPI × cycle time. Amdahl: 1/((1−f)+f/s).
Last-minute · must remember
  • Low address bits go to every chip (one chip’s worth: log₂n bits); the remaining high bits feed a decoder whose outputs are the chip selects. CS is usually active-low.
  • Two’s complement represents signed integers; the most significant bit acts as the sign bit.
  • Addressing modes determine how an instruction obtains its operand/effective address; distinguish immediate, direct, indirect, register, and indexed forms.
  • Cache mapping types: direct-mapped, fully associative, and set-associative.
  • DMA transfers blocks between I/O and memory with limited CPU involvement; interrupts notify the CPU of events.
  • Pipeline hazards: structural, data, and control hazards.
  • Know branch penalties, forwarding, stalling, and branch prediction at the level required for pipeline traces.
  • CNF can be built as a conjunction of clauses; DNF as a disjunction of terms. Use truth-table rows when algebra becomes error-prone.
  • Cayley–Hamilton: every square matrix satisfies its own characteristic polynomial; use it to reduce high powers of A.
  • For a connected undirected graph with n−1 edges, the graph is a tree; every tree edge is a bridge.
  • Convergence-test choice is pattern-driven: ratio/root for factorials and powers; comparison/integral for rational-like terms.
  • Instruction encoding problems are bit-budget problems: total instruction bits = opcode/function bits + operand/address bits.
  • For an S-way set-associative cache, number of sets = total cache lines / S.
  • Single-bus datapaths serialize bus transfers; multi-bus datapaths can read multiple sources concurrently.
  • Low-order interleaving maps word address A to bank A mod number_of_banks; high-order interleaving uses higher address bits.
  • Memory-mapped I/O shares the memory address space; isolated I/O uses a separate I/O address space.
  • Write-back + write-allocate vs write-through + no-write-allocate. Miss types: compulsory, capacity, conflict (none in a fully associative cache).
  • Daisy chain: nearest device has highest priority. DMA modes: burst blocks the CPU, cycle stealing takes one word at a time, transparent uses idle bus cycles. Memory-mapped I/O uses ordinary load/store.
  • Single-bus data path needs Y and Z registers (3 steps for R3 ← R1 + R2); three-bus does it in 1 step. Endianness: big-endian stores the most significant byte first.
Last-minute · common traps
  • Partial address decoding leaves unused high bits as don’t-cares, so one chip answers at several address ranges (aliasing). Full decoding gives each location a unique address.
  • Overflow in signed fixed-width arithmetic is distinct from carry out of the most significant bit.
  • AMAT depends on hit time and miss penalty; do not treat cache hit rate as the only performance factor.
  • Zero covariance does not generally imply independence. For independence, factor the joint distribution into marginals.
  • Endianness changes byte order across addresses; it does not reverse the bit order inside each byte.
  • Forwarding does not eliminate every pipeline stall; a load-use RAW hazard may still require a bubble depending on the pipeline.
  • Only RAW hazards stall a simple in-order pipeline; WAR and WAW need out-of-order execution. A load-use pair still costs 1 stall even with forwarding.
Last-minute · GATE favourites
  • Data hazards include RAW, WAR, and WAW; in a simple in-order pipeline, RAW is the central true dependency hazard.
  • For a finite set, equivalence relations correspond exactly to partitions of the set.
  • For binomial X~Bin(n,p): E=np and Var=np(1−p). For Poisson(λ): E=Var=λ.
  • Use complement for 'at least one', inclusion–exclusion for overlapping cases, and pigeonhole for guaranteed repetition.
  • Expanding opcode questions are best solved with a prefix-tree/code-space view, not by blindly counting all bit patterns.
  • Amdahl speedup = 1/[(1−f)+f/s], where f is the original execution-time fraction improved by factor s.
  • DMA transfers blocks between an I/O device and memory with much less CPU involvement than programmed I/O.

1C Programming Fundamentals

Common trap
Scope of this assumption
These sizes are a convention for the examples and practice questions in this hub, not a property of C. The C standard only guarantees sizeof(char) == 1, minimum value ranges (int ≥ 16 bits, long ≥ 32 bits, long long ≥ 64 bits) and the ordering short ≤ int ≤ long ≤ long long. In a real GATE question, use the machine assumptions the question gives (e.g. “4-byte int”, “8-byte pointers”) and do not carry the numbers below into other problems.

Data types and sizes

Reference table
TypeSize assumed in this sectionNotes
char1 bytesizeof(char) is always 1; may be signed or unsigned (implementation-defined)
short / int2 / 4 bytesint is the "natural" word size
long / long long8 (Linux 64-bit) / 8 byteslong is 4 bytes on some platforms — read the question
float / double4 / 8 bytesIEEE 754 single / double
pointer8 bytes (64-bit)size does not depend on the type pointed to
Notes

Unsigned arithmetic wraps modulo 2n. Signed overflow is undefined behaviour. In mixed signed/unsigned expressions the signed operand is converted to unsigned.

Operators, precedence and evaluation

Rules & facts
  • Integer division truncates toward zero (C99): 7/2 = 3, -7/2 = -3, 7/-2 = -3. The remainder takes the sign of the dividend: -7 % 2 = -1. Always (a/b)*b + a%b == a.
  • Precedence (high → low, simplified): () [] -> . → unary ! ~ ++ -- * & sizeof → * / % → + - → << >> → < <= > >= → == != → & → ^ → | → && → || → ?: → assignment → ,.
  • Relational operators are left-associative and yield 0 or 1, so 5 > 3 > 1 is (5 > 3) > 1 = 1 > 1 = 0.
  • Short-circuit: in a && b b is evaluated only if a is true; in a || b only if a is false. So if (i++ && j++) with i = 0, j = 0 leaves i = 1, j = 0.
  • Prefix ++i increments then yields the new value; postfix i++ yields the old value then increments.
  • Undefined behaviour
    Modifying a variable twice between sequence points — i = i++ + ++i, a[i] = i++, printf("%d %d", i++, i) — is undefined or unspecified. GATE usually avoids it; if an option says "undefined/compiler dependent", it is often right.
Worked example
Worked numerical — integer and unsigned semantics
unsigned char c = 200;
c = c + 100;                   // 300 mod 256 = 44                     → prints 44
printf("%d", -1 < 1u);          // -1 converted to unsigned = 4294967295 → comparison false → 0
printf("%d", -7/2 + -7%2);     // -3 + (-1) = -4
Output of the three prints: 44, 0, −4 (verified by compiling and running).
Worked example
Worked numerical — switch fall-through and loops
int k = 3;
switch (k) { case 1: printf("one ");  case 3: printf("three ");  case 4: printf("four "); break;  default: printf("def "); }
// prints:  three four       (no break after case 3, so execution falls into case 4)

for (i = 0; i < 5; i++) { if (i == 2) continue; if (i == 4) break; s += i; }
// s = 0 + 1 + 3 = 4, and i ends at 4

Bitwise operations

Key point
Handy identities
x & (x-1)   clears the lowest set bit
x & -x       isolates the lowest set bit
x << k       = x · 2ᵏ    (no overflow)
x >> k       = ⌊x / 2ᵏ⌋  (unsigned)
x ^ x = 0,   x ^ 0 = x
a ^= b; b ^= a; a ^= b;   swaps without temp
Key point
Counting set bits (Kernighan)
while (x) { x &= x - 1; count++; }
// 0x2D = 101101  → 4 iterations
// 40 & 39 = 32  (101000 → 100000)

Storage classes and scope

Reference table
ClassLifetime / scope
auto (default for locals)created on entry to the block, destroyed on exit; stack; uninitialised = garbage
registerhint to keep in a register; you cannot take its address
static (local)lives for the whole program, initialised once (to 0 by default), keeps its value between calls; scope is still the block
static (file level)internal linkage — visible only inside that file
externdeclares a global defined elsewhere; globals default to 0
Worked example
Worked numerical — static variable
int cnt() { static int n = 0; return ++n; }
cnt(); cnt(); printf("%d", cnt());      // static n persists → 3

The preprocessor

Notes

Macros are textual substitution before compilation — no type checking, no evaluation of arguments. Always parenthesise arguments and the whole body.

Worked example
Worked numerical — macro pitfalls
#define SQ(x) x*x
SQ(2+3)       →  2+3*2+3 = 11            (not 25)
100 / SQ(5)   →  100/5*5 = 20*5 = 100   (not 4)
#define SQ2(x) ((x)*(x))   →  SQ2(2+3) = 25   ✔

2Pointers, Arrays, Strings & Memory

Pointer arithmetic

Rules & facts
  • For T *p, p + k advances by k · sizeof(T) bytes. p - q (same array) gives the number of elements between them, not bytes.
  • a[i] is defined as *(a + i), so i[a] works too: for int arr[] = {1,2,3,4}, 2[arr] = 3.
  • The array name decays to a pointer to its first element in most expressions — but sizeof(a) gives the whole array (20 bytes for int a[5]), while for a function parameter int a[] is really int *a, so sizeof(a) there is 8.
  • An array name is not an lvalue: a = p; is illegal, a++ is illegal.
Key point
Pre/postfix with pointers
*p++    value at p, then p moves  (postfix binds tighter than *)
(*p)++  increments the VALUE
*++p    moves p first, then reads
++*p    increments the value, yields new value
Key point
Dereference and address
int x = 5, *p = &x, **pp = &p;
*p = 7;     // x is 7
**pp = 9;   // x is 9
*(&x) == x
Worked example
Worked numerical — traversing with a pointer
int a[] = {10, 20, 30, 40, 50};  int *p = a;
int v1 = *p++;     // v1 = 10, p now points to a[1]
int v2 = *p;       // v2 = 20
int v3 = ++*p;     // a[1] becomes 21, v3 = 21
// p - a = 1, and a[1] is now 21         → v1, v2, v3 = 10, 20, 21

Two-dimensional arrays

Notes

C stores 2-D arrays in row-major order. For int m[R][C]: &m[i][j] = base + (i·C + j)·sizeof(int), and m[i][j] == *(*(m + i) + j). Here m + 1 advances a whole row (C elements), whereas *m + 1 advances one element.

Worked example
Worked numerical
int m[3][4]: &m[2][1] − &m[0][0] = 2·4 + 1 = 9 elements. sizeof(m) = 48, and sizeof(m)/sizeof(m[0]) = 3 rows.

Call by value and pointers

Notes

C passes everything by value. A function receives a copy, so swapping two ints requires passing their addresses:

Formula / working
void swapv(int a, int b) { int t = a; a = b; b = t; }      // swaps the copies only
void swapp(int *a, int *b) { int t = *a; *a = *b; *b = t; } // swaps the caller's variables
// u = 3, w = 4:  swapv(u, w) → still 3 4;   swapp(&u, &w) → now 4 3

Strings

Rules & facts
  • A string is a char array ending with '\0'. strlen does not count the terminator, sizeof of an array does.
  • char s[] = "GATE"; → a writable array: sizeof(s) = 5, strlen(s) = 4. char *t = "GATE"; → a pointer to a read-only literal: sizeof(t) = 8, and modifying t[0] is undefined behaviour.
  • Comparing strings with == compares addresses; use strcmp. strcpy needs a destination of at least strlen+1 bytes.

Structures, unions and padding

Notes

Each member is aligned to its own size; the whole struct is padded to a multiple of its largest alignment. Member order matters. A union shares one block of memory; its size is the largest member, rounded up to the alignment.

Worked example
Worked numerical — sizeof with padding (verified with gcc)
struct A { char c; int i; char d; };        c(1) + pad(3) + i(4) + d(1) + pad(3)   = 12
struct B { int i; char c; char d; };        i(4) + c(1) + d(1) + pad(2)            = 8
struct C { char c; double d; int i; };      c(1) + pad(7) + d(8) + i(4) + pad(4)   = 24
union  U { int i; char c[6]; };             max(4, 6) = 6, rounded to alignment 4   = 8
Reordering members from largest to smallest alignment usually minimises padding.

Dynamic memory

Reference table
FunctionBehaviour
malloc(n)n uninitialised bytes from the heap; NULL on failure
calloc(k, n)k·n bytes, zero-initialised
realloc(p, n)resize; may move the block (old pointer becomes invalid)
free(p)returns the block; free(NULL) is safe; freeing twice is undefined
Rules & facts
  • Memory leak: allocated memory that is no longer reachable and never freed.
  • Dangling pointer: pointer to memory that was freed, or to a local variable that has gone out of scope (returning &local from a function).
  • Wild/uninitialised pointer: never assigned; dereferencing it is undefined. Set pointers to NULL after free.
  • Memory layout: code (text), static/global data, heap (grows up), stack (grows down).
Function pointers
Formula / working
int (*op)(int, int) = add;   // pointer to a function returning int taking two ints
int r = op(2, 3);            // calls add(2, 3);   used for callbacks, qsort's comparator

3Functions & Recursion

How a call works

Notes

Each call pushes an activation record (stack frame) holding the parameters, the return address, saved registers and local variables; return pops it. Recursion works because every invocation has its own frame. Stack depth = number of active (not yet returned) calls — infinite recursion ends in stack overflow.

Rules & facts
  • Parameters are passed by value (copies). Locals are re-created on every call; static locals and globals are shared across all invocations.
  • The order in which operands of +, function arguments, etc. are evaluated is unspecified in C — avoid questions that depend on it (for example, an expression that reads a global that a recursive call in the same expression modifies).
  • Tail recursion: the recursive call is the very last action (return f(n-1, acc*n);); a compiler can reuse the frame, turning it into a loop with O(1) stack. return n * f(n-1); is not tail recursive (a multiplication follows the call).

Tracing technique

Rules & facts
  1. Write the call tree. Mark what is printed before the recursive calls (pre-order) and after them (post-order).
  2. Evaluate from the base case upward; keep local variables separate per call.
  3. For counting problems write a recurrence for the number of calls and solve it.
Worked example
Worked numerical — print between two recursive calls
void ruler(int n) { if (n == 0) return;  ruler(n-1);  printf("%d ", n);  ruler(n-1); }
ruler(3):   ruler(2) prints  1 2 1;  then 3;  then ruler(2) prints 1 2 1
Output:  1 2 1 3 1 2 1

Calls: C(0) = 1,  C(n) = 2·C(n−1) + 1  →  C(n) = 2ⁿ⁺¹ − 1  →  C(3) = 15 calls
Number of values printed = 2ⁿ − 1 = 7

Classic recursive functions

Reference table
FunctionRecurrenceResult
factorialn! = n · (n−1)!n calls deep → O(n) time, O(n) stack
gcd(a, b)gcd(b, a mod b); gcd(a, 0) = agcd(48, 18) → gcd(18, 12) → gcd(12, 6) → gcd(6, 0) = 6
Fibonacci (naive)F(n) = F(n−1) + F(n−2)exponential time Θ(φⁿ); stack depth only n
Tower of HanoiH(n) = 2H(n−1) + 12ⁿ − 1 moves: 3 disks → 7, 10 disks → 1023
Binary search (recursive)T(n) = T(n/2) + 1O(log n) time and stack
McCarthy 91f(n) = n−10 if n > 100, else f(f(n+11))f(n) = 91 for every n ≤ 100: f(50) = f(99) = 91
Worked example
Worked numerical — number of calls in naive Fibonacci
Let C(n) = calls made by fib(n): C(0) = C(1) = 1 and C(n) = C(n−1) + C(n−2) + 1. 
C(2) = 3   C(3) = 5   C(4) = 9   C(5) = 15   C(6) = 25
In general C(n) = 2·F(n+1) − 1.  fib(5) returns 5 and makes 15 calls (counted by running the program).
Many of these calls recompute the same value — the reason dynamic programming and memoisation turn the time into O(n).
GATE favourite
GATE angle
Three things to check every time: (1) what is printed before vs after the recursive call; (2) static / global state shared across calls; (3) the base case — a missing or unreachable base case means infinite recursion. Also be ready to give the space complexity as the maximum depth of the recursion, not the number of calls.

4Arrays, Stacks & Queues

Arrays

Reference table
OperationUnsorted arraySorted array
Access by indexO(1)O(1)
SearchO(n)O(log n) (binary search)
Insert / delete at a positionO(n) (shift elements); O(1) at the endO(n)
Notes

Address of element i of a 1-D array = base + (i − lower)·w; 2-D addressing is as in the pointer section. Sparse matrices are stored as triples (row, col, value) or in CSR form to save space.

Stack (LIFO)

Notes

Operations push, pop, peek, isEmpty all take O(1). Uses: function calls, undo, expression evaluation, balanced-bracket checking, DFS, backtracking.

Rules & facts
  • Two stacks in one array of size n: start one at index 0 growing up, the other at n−1 growing down; overflow only when they meet — the whole array is usable.
  • Stack permutations: if 1, 2, …, n are pushed in order and popped at any time, the number of possible pop sequences is the Catalan number C(n) = (1/(n+1))·C(2n, n): n = 3 → 5 (only 3 1 2 is impossible), n = 4 → 14.
Infix → postfix with a stack
Notes

Scan left to right. Operands go straight to the output. For an operator, pop operators of higher or equal precedence (for left-associative operators; for the right-associative ^ only strictly higher) to the output, then push it. ( is pushed; ) pops until (. At the end pop everything.

Worked example
Worked numerical — conversions
A + B * C - D / E        →  A B C * + D E / -          (max stack size 2)
A * (B + C) / D - E      →  A B C + * D / E -          (max stack size 3, counting the parenthesis)
(A + B) * (C - D)        →  A B + C D - *
Postfix evaluation
Notes

Operands are pushed; an operator pops two values (right operand first), applies the operation and pushes the result. The stack depth needed is the maximum number of pending operands.

Worked example
Worked numerical
5 6 2 + * 12 4 / -: 
push 5, 6, 2     [5 6 2]
+                [5 8]
*                [40]
push 12, 4       [40 12 4]
/                [40 3]
-                [37]       → result 37   (maximum stack size 3)

Queue (FIFO)

Notes

Operations enqueue at the rear, dequeue from the front — O(1) with a linked list or a circular array. Uses: BFS, scheduling, buffering, level-order traversal.

Rules & facts
  • Circular array queue of size n (indices mod n), with front = index of the first element and rear = index of the next free slot, one slot kept empty: 
    empty : front == rear
full  : (rear + 1) % n == front        → capacity n − 1
count : (rear − front + n) % n
    Without the "wasted slot" trick, front == rear is ambiguous between empty and full, so a separate counter is needed.
  • Queue using two stacks: push into stack 1; to dequeue, if stack 2 is empty move everything from stack 1 over (reversing order), then pop stack 2. Each element moves at most once → amortised O(1) per operation. A stack using two queues needs one operation to cost O(n).
  • Deque allows insertion/deletion at both ends; a priority queue removes the highest-priority element (implemented with a heap).
Worked example
Worked numerical
Circular queue with n = 8, front = 5, rear = 2: count = (2 − 5 + 8) mod 8 = 5 elements (at indices 5, 6, 7, 0, 1).
Common trap
Remember
BFS uses a queue; iterative DFS uses a stack (recursion uses the system call stack). Minimum number of stacks needed to simulate a queue = 2; minimum number of queues to simulate a stack = 2.

5Linked Lists

Node structure and types

Formula / working
struct node { int data;  struct node *next; };       // singly linked
struct dnode { int data; struct dnode *prev, *next; }; // doubly linked
Rules & facts
  • Singly: traverse one way only. Doubly: both ways; can delete a given node in O(1). Circular: last node points back to the first (no NULL); the traversal condition is p->next != head.
  • Use a dummy (sentinel) head node to avoid special cases for the first element.
  • A list with n nodes holds n next pointers (singly) or 2n pointers (doubly), plus the head pointer.

Complexity

Reference table
OperationSingly (head only)Singly (head + tail)Doubly
Insert at frontO(1)O(1)O(1)
Insert at endO(n)O(1)O(1) with tail
Delete firstO(1)O(1)O(1)
Delete lastO(n) (need predecessor)O(n)O(1) with tail
Delete a node given a pointer to itO(n) normally (O(1) trick below)–O(1)
Search / access k-thO(n) — binary search is not possible on a linked list in O(log n)
Notes

O(1) deletion trick (node is not the last): copy the next node's data into the given node, then unlink and free the next node.

Essential algorithms

Formula / Rule
Reverse a list (iterative, O(n) time, O(1) space)
prev = NULL; cur = head;
while (cur) {
  nxt = cur->next;
  cur->next = prev;
  prev = cur;  cur = nxt;
}
head = prev;
Performs exactly n pointer reversals.
Formula / Rule
Middle / cycle (two pointers)
slow = fast = head;
while (fast && fast->next) {
  slow = slow->next;
  fast = fast->next->next;   // meets slow iff a cycle exists
}
// no cycle: slow is the middle
Floyd's cycle detection: O(n) time, O(1) space.
Rules & facts
  • k-th node from the end: advance one pointer k steps, then move both until the first hits NULL — one pass.
  • Merge two sorted lists: O(m + n) time, O(1) extra space. Merge sort on a linked list: O(n log n) time (and it is the preferred sort for lists). Insertion sort works on a list in O(n²).
  • Polynomial and large-integer arithmetic, adjacency lists and hash-chaining all rely on linked lists. An XOR linked list stores prev ⊕ next in one field to halve the pointer cost.
Worked example
Worked numerical — recursion on a list
void fun(struct node *h) {
    if (!h) return;
    printf("%d ", h->data);
    if (h->next) fun(h->next->next);
    printf("%d ", h->data);
}
List: 1 → 2 → 3 → 4 → 5          fun(head) prints:  1 3 5 5 3 1
fun(1) prints 1, jumps to 3; fun(3) prints 3, jumps to 5; fun(5) prints 5, next is NULL so no call;
then each frame prints its value again on the way back: 5, 3, 1.
Worked example
Worked numerical — delete alternate nodes
Keep a node, unlink its successor, move to the node after: on 1→2→3→4→5 the loop leaves 1→3→5; on 1→2→3→4→5→6 it also leaves 1→3→5.
Common trap
Array vs linked list
Array: random access O(1), contiguous, cache-friendly, fixed or costly resize, insertion O(n). Linked list: insertion/deletion O(1) once the position is known, no resize cost, extra pointer memory per node, no random access.

6Trees, BST & AVL

Binary tree facts

Notes

Take the root at level 0 and the height of a single node as 0 (height = number of edges on the longest root–leaf path). Be careful: some problems count levels from 1 — read the convention.

Rules & facts
  • Maximum nodes at level i = 2i. Maximum nodes in a tree of height h = 2h+1 − 1 (h = 3 → 15). Minimum height of n nodes = ⌊log₂ n⌋.
  • A binary tree with n nodes has n − 1 edges and n + 1 NULL child pointers.
  • In any binary tree: (leaves) = (nodes with 2 children) + 1.
  • For a tree in which every internal node has exactly k children: with i internal nodes, n = k·i + 1 nodes and (k−1)·i + 1 leaves. With k = 3 and i = 10 → 21 leaves. A full binary tree with i internal nodes has i + 1 leaves.
  • Number of structurally different binary trees with n nodes = Catalan(n) = C(2n, n)/(n+1): 1, 2, 5, 14, 42 for n = 1…5. The same number of distinct BSTs can be built on n distinct keys: 4 keys → 14.
  • Complete tree in an array (0-based): children of i are 2i+1 and 2i+2, parent is ⌊(i−1)/2⌋ (1-based: 2i, 2i+1, ⌊i/2⌋).

Traversals

Reference table
TraversalOrderTypical use
InorderLeft, Node, Rightsorted output of a BST
PreorderNode, Left, Rightcopy a tree; prefix expression
PostorderLeft, Right, Nodedelete a tree; postfix expression; evaluate expression trees
Level orderlevel by level (queue)BFS, shortest depth
Rules & facts
  • All recursive traversals take O(n) time and O(h) stack space. Morris traversal does inorder in O(1) space using threads.
  • Unique reconstruction: inorder + preorder, or inorder + postorder, determine a binary tree uniquely. Preorder + postorder do not (unless every node has 0 or 2 children). The first preorder element (or last postorder element) is the root; split the inorder sequence around it.

Binary search tree (BST)

Notes

Left subtree keys < node < right subtree keys. Search, insert and delete cost O(h): O(log n) when balanced, O(n) when skewed (e.g. keys inserted in sorted order). The inorder traversal of a BST is sorted.

Deletion: (1) leaf → remove; (2) one child → replace by that child; (3) two children → replace the key by its inorder successor (minimum of the right subtree) or predecessor, then delete that node.

Worked example
Worked numerical — build, traverse, delete
Insert 50, 30, 70, 20, 40, 60, 80, 35 into an empty BST. 
        50
      /    \
    30      70
   /  \    /  \
  20  40  60  80
      /
    35
Inorder  : 20 30 35 40 50 60 70 80        (sorted)
Preorder : 50 30 20 40 35 70 60 80
Postorder: 20 35 40 30 60 80 70 50        height = 3

Delete 30 (two children): successor = min of right subtree {40, 35} = 35 → 35 replaces 30.
   Preorder now: 50 35 20 40 70 60 80
Delete 50 (root, two children): successor = min of {70, 60, 80} = 60 → 60 becomes the root.
   Preorder now: 60 35 20 40 70 80

AVL tree (height-balanced)

Notes

A BST in which the balance factor (height of left − height of right) of every node is −1, 0 or +1. After an insert or delete, rebalance with rotations at the lowest unbalanced node:

Reference table
CaseCauseFix
LLinsertion into left subtree of left childone right rotation
RRinsertion into right subtree of right childone left rotation
LRinsertion into right subtree of left childleft rotation on the child, then right rotation
RLinsertion into left subtree of right childright rotation on the child, then left rotation
Rules & facts
  • Height is always O(log n) (≤ 1.44 log₂ n), so search/insert/delete are O(log n). Insertion needs at most one (single or double) rotation; deletion may need O(log n) rotations.
  • Minimum nodes of an AVL tree of height h: N(h) = N(h−1) + N(h−2) + 1 with N(0) = 1, N(1) = 2: 1, 2, 4, 7, 12, 20, 33, 54 for h = 0…7. So height 4 needs at least 12 nodes.
Worked example
Worked numerical — AVL insertions
Insert 10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 25: 
10, 20, 30   → right-right imbalance at 10         → left rotation          root 20 (10, 30)
40           → fits                                  
50           → right-right imbalance at 30         → left rotation          20 (10, 40(30, 50))
25           → under 30's left; imbalance at 20 is right-left → double rotation
Final tree:      30
               /    \
             20      40
            /  \       \
          10   25       50           preorder 30 20 10 25 40 50, height 2
A plain (unbalanced) BST with the same insertions has height 4 and degenerates to a list-like shape.

7Heaps, Hashing & Graph Representation

Binary heap

Notes

A complete binary tree stored in an array. Max-heap: every parent ≥ its children (the maximum is at the root); min-heap: every parent ≤ its children. With 1-based indexing: left child 2i, right child 2i+1, parent ⌊i/2⌋. A heap with n nodes has height ⌊log₂ n⌋ and ⌈n/2⌉ leaves (the last ⌈n/2⌉ array positions).

Reference table
OperationTimeHow
Find max (min-heap: min)O(1)root
InsertO(log n)place at the end, sift up while larger than the parent
Delete maxO(log n)move the last element to the root, sift down (swap with the larger child)
Build heap from n elementsO(n)sift down every internal node from ⌊n/2⌋ down to 1 (inserting one by one costs O(n log n))
HeapsortO(n log n)build max-heap, then repeatedly swap the root to the end and sift down; in place, not stable
Search for an arbitrary keyO(n)a heap is not ordered across subtrees
Find the minimum in a max-heapO(n)it is one of the leaves
Worked example
Worked numerical — bottom-up build and one insertion
Build a max-heap from [4, 10, 3, 5, 1]   (n = 5, internal nodes at positions 2 and 1)
 sift-down position 2 (10): children 5, 1 → fine
 sift-down position 1 (4):  swap with 10 → [10, 4, 3, 5, 1];  4 then swaps with 5 → [10, 5, 3, 4, 1]
 Result: [10, 5, 3, 4, 1]   (2 swaps)

Insert 14 into the max-heap [15, 10, 12, 8, 9, 4, 3]:
 append → [15, 10, 12, 8, 9, 4, 3, 14];  14 > parent 8 → swap;  14 > parent 10 → swap;  14 < 15 stop
 Result: [15, 14, 12, 10, 9, 4, 3, 8]   (2 swaps)

Delete max from [20, 15, 12, 8, 9, 4, 3]: root ← last (3) → sift down: 3 ↔ 15, then 3 ↔ 9 → [15, 9, 12, 8, 3, 4]
Formula / Rule
Worked numerical — inserting 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 one by one into a max-heap
Each new key is larger than everything before, so it climbs to the root: swaps = 0 + 1 + 1 + 2 + 2 = 6, final array [5, 4, 2, 1, 3]. Building the same heap bottom-up from [1,2,3,4,5] needs fewer swaps — one reason build-heap is O(n) rather than O(n log n).

Hashing

Notes

A hash function h(k) maps a key to a slot in a table of size m. With n keys the load factor is α = n / m. A collision happens when two keys hash to the same slot.

Reference table
MethodHow collisions are handledNotes
Chainingeach slot holds a linked listα can exceed 1; average search ≈ 1 + α; worst O(n)
Linear probingtry h(k)+1, +2, … (mod m)simple, cache friendly; suffers primary clustering
Quadratic probingtry h(k)+1², +2², …avoids primary clustering; may not visit every slot
Double hashingtry h₁(k) + i·h₂(k)closest to uniform hashing; h₂(k) must be non-zero and coprime to m
Rules & facts
  • Open addressing stores everything in the table, so n ≤ m (α ≤ 1). Expected probes for an unsuccessful search ≈ 1/(1 − α). Deleting a key must leave a tombstone marker, otherwise later searches stop too early.
  • Good hash functions spread keys uniformly; the division method h(k) = k mod m works best with m prime and not close to a power of 2.
  • Average search/insert/delete is O(1); the worst case is O(n). Hash tables do not support ordered operations (min, range queries) efficiently.
Worked example
Worked numerical — collision resolution (m = 10, h(k) = k mod 10)
Insert 31, 12, 41, 22, 32, 13. 
Linear probing:    31→1   12→2   41→1✗2✗→3   22→2✗3✗→4   32→2✗3✗4✗→5   13→3✗4✗5✗→6
   table: [_, 31, 12, 41, 22, 32, 13, _, _, _]       probes: 1, 1, 3, 3, 4, 4  (total 16)
Quadratic (+i²):   table: [_, 31, 12, 22, 13, 41, 32, _, _, _]      (41 lands at slot 1+4 = 5)
Chaining:          slot 1: 31 → 41     slot 2: 12 → 22 → 32     slot 3: 13
Note how linear probing builds one long cluster (slots 1–6) — new keys hashing anywhere into it must probe to its end.
Worked example
Worked numerical — load factor
1000 keys in a chained table of 200 slots: α = 1000 / 200 = 5 — the average chain holds 5 keys.

Graph representations

Reference table
Adjacency matrixAdjacency list
SpaceO(V²)O(V + E)
Is (u, v) an edge?O(1)O(deg u)
List all neighbours of uO(V)O(deg u)
BFS / DFS of the whole graphO(V²)O(V + E)
Best fordense graphssparse graphs
Rules & facts
  • Undirected graph → symmetric matrix; the sum of all matrix entries = 2E. Matrix powers: entry (i, j) of Ak counts walks of length k.
  • BFS uses a queue and visits by increasing distance; DFS uses a stack (or recursion).
Worked example
Worked numerical — traversal order
Graph with edges 0–1, 0–2, 1–3, 1–4, 2–5, 4–5 (neighbours visited in increasing order), starting at 0: 
BFS order: 0 1 2 3 4 5          DFS order: 0 1 3 4 5 2
Edges = 6; adjacency matrix has 36 cells; adjacency lists hold 12 entries.

8Problem → Standard Method Playbook

1. Pointer declarators: array of pointers vs pointer to array

Method
Method: start at the identifier and read the declaration outward, respecting parentheses. int *p[5] means an array of 5 pointers to int; int (*p)[5] means a pointer to an array of 5 int. For function pointers, parentheses around *name are essential.
Worked example
Worked problem — declaration parsing
Q: What is int (*p)[10]?
Method notes

Answer: p is a pointer to an array of 10 integers. Thus p+1 advances by 10*sizeof(int) bytes, not by one integer.

9Last-minute one-liners

Last-minute · formulas & rules
  • For an array a with n elements, valid indices are 0 through n-1.
  • A binary tree with height h has at most 2h+1-1 nodes when root height is 0.
Last-minute · must remember
  • In C, arrays are contiguous objects; pointer arithmetic is scaled by the pointed-to type.
  • String literals are not modifiable; char arrays initialised from literals are distinct from pointers to string literals.
  • Call-by-value passes a value; C does not have native call-by-reference, but pointers can be used to modify caller-visible objects.
  • Recursion needs a correct base case and progress toward it; analyse recurrence/stack depth separately from total work.
  • Stack follows LIFO; queue follows FIFO.
  • A linked list provides dynamic node-based storage but loses direct O(1) random indexing.
  • BST inorder traversal produces keys in sorted order when the BST invariant holds.
  • AVL trees maintain a balance-factor constraint, keeping height O(log n).
  • A binary heap is a complete binary tree satisfying heap order; build-heap can be done in O(n).
  • Adjacency matrix uses Θ(V²) space; adjacency lists use Θ(V+E) space for sparse graphs.
  • Read C declarations from the identifier outward: int *p[5] is an array of pointers; int (*p)[5] is a pointer to an array.
  • Integer promotions happen before the usual arithmetic conversions determine the common operand type.
  • Morris inorder traversal temporarily threads predecessor links and uses O(1) auxiliary space.
  • Bit-field layout is partly implementation-defined; follow the assumptions stated by the question.
  • A heap is a complete tree with heap order; it is not a BST.
Last-minute · common traps
  • Array names usually decay to pointers to the first element in expressions, but an array is not itself a pointer.
  • Heap is not a BST: heap order does not imply globally sorted left/right subtrees.
  • volatile does not provide atomicity or inter-thread synchronization.
